F_{1}\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\mathsf{A}}(X)
which is different from
F
, then there are two possibilities:

•

F_{1}
is contained in
F^{\prime}
, or

•

F_{1}
is contained in
F^{\prime}_{1}\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}(X)
such that
F_{1}^{\prime}\neq F^{\prime}
.

In the first case,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}_{\delta\xi}(\gamma)|_{F_{1}}=e^{\mathsf{T}}_{K}(N^{-}_{F^{\prime}/X})\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}_{\delta\xi}(\gamma)|_{F_{1}},

then we have

\displaystyle\deg_{\xi}\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\xi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\circ\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}_{\delta\xi}(\gamma)|_{F_{1}}=\deg_{\xi}\left(e^{\mathsf{T}}_{K}(N^{-}_{F^{\prime}/X})\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}_{\delta\xi}(\gamma)|_{F_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle\subsetneq\deg_{\xi}\left(e^{\mathsf{T}}_{K}(N^{-}_{F^{\prime}/X})e^{\mathsf{T}}_{K}(N^{-}_{F_{1}/F^{\prime}})\right)+\mathrm{weight}_{\delta\xi}\left(\det(N^{-}_{F_{1}/F^{\prime}})^{1/2}\otimes\mathsf{s}^{\prime}|_{F_{1}}\right)-\mathrm{weight}_{\delta\xi}\left(\det(N^{-}_{F/F^{\prime}})^{1/2}\otimes\mathsf{s}^{\prime}|_{F}\right)

\displaystyle=\deg_{\xi}e^{\mathsf{T}}_{K}(N^{-}_{F_{1}/X})+\mathrm{weight}_{\xi}\left(\det(N^{-}_{F_{1}/X})^{1/2}\otimes\mathsf{s}|_{F_{1}}\right)-\mathrm{weight}_{\xi}\left(\det(N^{-}_{F/X})^{1/2}\otimes\mathsf{s}|_{F}\right).

In the second case, we have

\displaystyle\deg_{\xi^{\prime}}\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}_{\delta\xi}(\gamma)|_{F_{1}}

\displaystyle\subseteq\deg_{\xi^{\prime}}\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}_{\delta\xi}(\gamma)|_{F^{\prime}_{1}}

by axiom (iii) of
\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\xi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\subsetneq\deg_{\xi^{\prime}}e^{\mathsf{T}}_{K}(N^{-}_{F^{\prime}_{1}/X})+\mathrm{weight}_{\xi^{\prime}}\left(\det(N^{-}_{F^{\prime}_{1}/X})^{1/2}\otimes\mathsf{s}|_{F^{\prime}_{1}}\right)-\mathrm{weight}_{\xi^{\prime}}\left(\det(N^{-}_{F^{\prime}/X})^{1/2}\otimes\mathsf{s}|_{F^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=\deg_{\xi^{\prime}}e^{\mathsf{T}}_{K}(N^{-}_{F_{1}/X})+\mathrm{weight}_{\xi^{\prime}}\left(\det(N^{-}_{F_{1}/X})^{1/2}\otimes\mathsf{s}|_{F_{1}}\right)-\mathrm{weight}_{\xi^{\prime}}\left(\det(N^{-}_{F/X})^{1/2}\otimes\mathsf{s}|_{F}\right).

Note that for two Laurent polynomials
f
and
g
, if
\deg_{\xi^{\prime}}f\subsetneq\deg_{\xi^{\prime}}g
, then

\deg_{\xi}f\subsetneq\deg_{\xi}g
for small perturbation
\xi=\xi^{\prime}+\delta\xi
. Applying the observation to

f=\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}_{\delta\xi}(\gamma)|_{F_{1}},\,\,\,g=e^{\mathsf{T}}_{K}(N^{-}_{F_{1}/X})\otimes\det(N^{-}_{F_{1}/X})^{1/2}\otimes\mathsf{s}|_{F_{1}}\otimes\det(N^{-}_{F/X})^{-1/2}\otimes\left(\mathsf{s}|_{F}\right)^{-1},

we see that our claim holds in the second case as well. Then it follows from the uniqueness of stable envelopes (Proposition
3.16
) that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}_{\delta\xi}(\gamma)=\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\xi}(\gamma).

The lemma then follows from
\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\xi}=\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
,
\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\xi^{\prime}}=\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}
, and
\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}_{\delta\xi}=\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{C}/\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}
.
∎

Remark 3.38
.

A key fact used in the proof of Lemma
3.37
is that small perturbation in
\xi\in\operatorname{Lie}(\mathsf{A})_{\mathbb{R}}
preserves the degree bound condition.
The corresponding cohomological statement is
not true
, as illustrated by the following example. Suppose that
\operatorname{Lie}(\mathsf{A})_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
with coordinate
(x,y)
, and let
\mathfrak{C}=\{x>0,y>0\}
and
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}=\{x>0,y=0\}
, and let
f=y^{2},g=x
. For the coweight
\xi^{\prime}
such that
\langle x,\xi^{\prime}\rangle=1,\langle y,\xi^{\prime}\rangle=0
, we have

0=\deg_{\xi^{\prime}}(f)<\deg_{\xi^{\prime}}(g)=1,

but for its perturbation
\xi
such that
\langle x,\xi\rangle=1,\langle y,\xi\rangle=\delta
, we have
2=\deg_{\xi}(f)>\deg_{\xi}(g)=1
. So we do not have a straightforward analogue of Lemma
3.37
for cohomological case.

Nevertheless, we show that the cohomological triangle lemma indeed holds for symmetric GIT quotients with any
torus invariant potential functions, see Theorem
4.16
.

3.10.
General flavour groups

Recall in Setting
2.1
, we take
\mathsf{T}
to be a torus. This is a simplifying condition and it can be relaxed to the following.

Setting 3.39
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety over
\mathbb{C}
with a linear algebraic group
\mathsf{F}
-action and
\mathsf{w}\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
be an
\mathsf{F}
-invariant regular function. Let
\mathsf{A}\subseteq Z(\mathsf{F})
be a torus which lies in the center
Z(\mathsf{F})
of
\mathsf{F}
.

The definition of stable envelopes (Definitions
3.4
,
3.10
) can be stated verbally with the torus
\mathsf{T}
replaced by linear algebraic group
\mathsf{F}
. Results displayed in above remain unchanged with
\mathsf{T}
replaced by
\mathsf{F}
.

Moreover, when stable envelopes exist, they are compatible with changing of flavour groups. Namely, if
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{F}
is a subgroup which contains
\mathsf{A}
, then the following diagram commutes:

(3.14)

\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{F}}(X^{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{w}^{\mathsf{A}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,\,\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}^{F}_{F^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{F}}(X,\mathsf{w})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}^{F}_{F^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{F}^{\prime}}(X^{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{w}^{\mathsf{A}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,\,\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}}
\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{F}^{\prime}}(X,\mathsf{w})}

where the vertical arrow is the restriction of coefficients map
\mathrm{res}^{F}_{F^{\prime}}\colon H^{\mathsf{F}}(\cdots)\to H^{\mathsf{F}^{\prime}}(\cdots)
. There is also a similar commutative diagram for critical
K
-theory:

(3.15)

\textstyle{K^{\mathsf{F}}(X^{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{w}^{\mathsf{A}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,\,\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}^{F}_{F^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{K^{\mathsf{F}}(X,\mathsf{w})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}^{F}_{F^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{K^{\mathsf{F}^{\prime}}(X^{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{w}^{\mathsf{A}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,\,\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}}
\textstyle{K^{\mathsf{F}^{\prime}}(X,\mathsf{w})}

We will use stable envelopes under Setting
3.39
to construct
interpolation maps in §
9
.

4.
Cohomological stable envelopes on symmetric GIT quotients

In this section, we show that cohomological stable envelope correspondences exist for symmetric GIT quotients,
given by the fundamental class of the closure
[\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}(\Delta)]
of attracting set of the diagonal
(Theorem
4.3
).

We prove the triangle lemma for corresponding stable envelopes (Theorem
4.16
).
This is done by introducing the so-called nonabelian stable envelopes (see Definition
4.18
and Theorem
4.19
for a characterization property), and a compatible diagram between stable envelopes and Hall operations (Theorem
4.22
).
For symmetric quiver varieties, a different proof of the existence of stable envelopes as well as the triangle lemma is discussed in
[
COZZ2
]
.

As a side application, we derive an explicit formula of cohomological stable envelopes when potentials are zero (Corollary
4.25
).

4.1.
Symmetric GIT quotients

Definition 4.1
.

Given the following data:

•

A connected complex reductive group
G
, a Cartan and a Borel subgroup
H\subseteq B\subseteq G
,

•

Two tori
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\mathsf{T}
,

•

A finite dimensional
(G\times\mathsf{T})
-representation
R
such that
R\cong R^{\vee}
as
(G\times\mathsf{A})
-representations,

•

\theta\colon G\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
such that the semistable locus equals the stable locus
R^{{\theta}\emph{-}ss}=R^{{\theta}\emph{-}s}\neq\emptyset
on which
G
-action is free,

we call
X:=R/\!\!/_{\theta}G=R^{{\theta}\emph{-}s}/G
a
symmetric GIT quotient
.
It is endowed with the induced action by tori
\mathsf{T}
and
\mathsf{A}
.

We also have the associated variety
\widehat{X}:=R^{{\theta}\emph{-}s}\times_{G}(G/H)
with a
G/H
-fibration
p\colon\widehat{X}\to X
.

Remark 4.2
.

For the induced character
\bar{\theta}\colon H\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
, there is an open immersion

\widehat{X}\cong R^{{\theta}\emph{-}s}/H\hookrightarrow R^{\bar{\theta}\emph{-}ss}/H
.

The main theorem of this section is:

Theorem 4.3
.

Given a symmetric GIT quotient
X
with an action by tori
\mathsf{T}
and
\mathsf{A}
as Definition
4.1
,
fix a chamber
\mathfrak{C}
. Then

\sum_{F\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\mathsf{A}}(X)}[\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{F})]\in H^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\operatorname{Attr}^{f}_{\mathfrak{C}}\right)

is a cohomological stable envelope correspondence for

(X,0,\mathsf{T},\mathsf{A},\mathfrak{C})
.

Our main examples of symmetric GIT quotients are given by symmetric quiver varieties introduced below.

4.2.
Quiver varieties

A
quiver

Q
is a pair of finite sets
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1})
together with two maps
h,t\colon Q_{1}\to Q_{0}
. We will call
Q_{0}
the set of nodes and
Q_{1}
the set of arrows and
h
(resp.
t
) sends an arrow to its head (resp. tail). If
a\in Q_{1}
, then we will write
t(a)\to h(a)
to denote the arrow
a
. We define the

adjacency matrix

\displaystyle(\mathsf{Q}_{ij})_{i,j\in Q_{0}}=\#(i\to j)

and
Cartan matrix

\displaystyle(\mathsf{C}_{ij})_{i,j\in Q_{0}}=2\delta_{ij}-\mathsf{Q}_{ij}-\mathsf{Q}_{ji}.

Take dimension vectors
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}
,

\underline{\mathbf{d}}=(\mathbf{d}_{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{d}_{\mathrm{out}})\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}
, the
space of framed representations
of
Q
with
gauge dimension

\mathbf{v}
and
in-coming framing dimension

\mathbf{d}_{\mathrm{in}}
and
out-going framing dimension

\mathbf{d}_{\mathrm{out}}
is

(4.1)

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}):=R(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})=\bigoplus_{a\in Q_{1}}\underbrace{\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{t(a)}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{h(a)}})}_{X_{a}}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in Q_{0}}\left(\underbrace{\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{\mathrm{in},i}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{i}})}_{A_{i}}\oplus\underbrace{\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{i}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}})}_{B_{i}}\right).

The
gauge group

G=\prod_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathrm{GL}(\mathbf{v}_{i})
naturally acts on
R(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
by compositions with maps.

Choose a stability condition
\theta\in\mathbb{Q}^{Q_{0}}
such that
\theta
-semistable representations are
\theta
-stable:

R(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})^{ss}=R(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})^{s}\neq\emptyset,

and define the
quiver variety
as the GIT quotient:

\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}):=\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}):=R(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})/\!\!/_{\theta}G=R(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})^{s}/G.

As we do not impose any relation on the quiver, the above space is a smooth quasi-projective variety.

We define an action of

(4.2)

\displaystyle G_{\mathrm{edge}}:=\prod_{i,j\in Q_{0}}\mathrm{GL}(\mathsf{Q}_{ij})

on
R(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
: given a pair of nodes
i,j\in Q_{0}
, the contribution of the edges from
i
to
j
is
\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{i}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{j}})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\mathsf{Q}_{ij}}
, then the factor
\mathrm{GL}(\mathsf{Q}_{ij})
naturally acts on the second component.

We also consider the following group actions on
R(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
:

(4.3)

\displaystyle G_{\mathrm{fram}}^{\mathrm{in}}=\prod_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathrm{GL}(\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in},i})\curvearrowright\bigoplus_{i\in Q_{0}}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in},i}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{i}}),\quad G_{\mathrm{fram}}^{\mathrm{out}}=\prod_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathrm{GL}(\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i})\curvearrowright\bigoplus_{i\in Q_{0}}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{i}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}}).

We define the
flavour group

(4.4)

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}:=G_{\mathrm{fram}}^{\mathrm{in}}\times G_{\mathrm{fram}}^{\mathrm{out}}\times G_{\mathrm{edge}}.

It is easy to see that
\mathsf{F}\cong\operatorname{Aut}_{G}(R(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}))
.

Definition 4.4
.

We say that a framing
\underline{\mathbf{d}}
is
symmetric
if
\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}}=\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out}}=\mathbf{d}
. In this case, we simplify the notations as

(4.5)

R(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})=R(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})=R(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}),\quad\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})=\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})=\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}),

and we define
G_{\mathrm{fram}}^{\mathrm{\operatorname{diag}}}\subseteq\mathsf{F}
to be the diagonal subgroup of
G_{\mathrm{fram}}^{\mathrm{in}}\times G_{\mathrm{fram}}^{\mathrm{out}}
.

Definition 4.5
.

We say
Q
is
symmetric
if its adjacency matrix
(\mathsf{Q}_{ij})_{i,j\in Q_{0}}
is symmetric.
For a symmetric quiver
Q
, we call the associated quiver variety
symmetric quiver variety
(SQV) if the framing is symmetric.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
be a symmetric quiver with symmetric framing as above. Suppose that there exists a torus
\mathsf{H}
together with a linear action of
\mathsf{H}
on
R(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
. We say that a linear action of a torus
\mathsf{H}
on
R(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
is
self-dual
if the
\mathsf{H}
-action commutes with the gauge group
G
-action, and
R(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
is self-dual as
(G\times\mathsf{H})
-representation.

We say that a torus action
\mathsf{H}
on the symmetric quiver variety
\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
is
self-dual
if it is induced from a self-dual action of
\mathsf{H}
on
R(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
.

Symmetric quiver varieties with self-dual torus actions are examples of Definition
4.1
, moreover their
\mathsf{A}
-fixed loci are also
symmetric quiver varieties as shown below.

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose that
X=\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
is a symmetric quiver variety, and
\mathsf{A}
is a torus with a self-dual action on
X
. Let
\sigma
be a cocharacter of
\mathsf{A}
, then the
\sigma
-fixed points locus
X^{\sigma}
is a disjoint union of symmetric quiver varieties. Moreover, the induced action of
\mathsf{A}
on
X^{\sigma}
is self-dual.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of
[
MO
, Prop. 2.3.1]
. In fact, the same argument as
loc. cit.
shows that

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})^{\sigma}=\bigsqcup_{\phi/\sim}\mathcal{M}_{\theta_{\phi}}(Q_{\phi},\mathbf{v}_{\phi},\mathbf{d}_{\phi}).

Here
\phi:\mathbb{C}^{*}\to G\times\mathsf{A}
is a lift of
\sigma:\mathbb{C}^{*}\to\mathsf{A}
along the projection
G\times\mathsf{A}\to\mathsf{A}
, and
(Q_{\phi},\mathbf{v}_{\phi},\mathbf{d}_{\phi})
is constructed in
[
MO
, §2.3.3]
, and two lifts
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
are equivalent (denoted as
\phi_{1}\sim\phi_{2}
) if they give the same action of
\mathsf{A}
on
R(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
. The disjoint unions on the right-hand-side of (
4.6
) are labelled by equivalent classes of lifts
\phi
. By construction,
R(Q_{\phi},\mathbf{v}_{\phi},\mathbf{d}_{\phi})
is the
\phi
-fixed points locus
R(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})^{\phi}
, and the gauge group for
Q_{\phi}
is the
\phi
-fixed points subgroup
G^{\phi}
. Since
R(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
is a self-dual
G\times\mathsf{A}
representation and
\mathbb{C}^{*}
acts on it through
\phi\colon\mathbb{C}^{*}\to G\times\mathsf{A}
, it follows that
R(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})^{\phi}
is a self-dual
G^{\phi}\times\mathsf{A}
representation. Hence
Q_{\phi}
is a symmetric quiver, and the induced
\mathsf{A}
-action on
\mathcal{M}_{\theta_{\phi}}(Q_{\phi},\mathbf{v}_{\phi},\mathbf{d}_{\phi})
is self-dual.
∎

4.3.
Generalities on attracting closures

Definition 4.8
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{A})
be in the Setting
2.1
and
\sigma
be a cocharacter of
\mathsf{A}
(not necessarily generic). Denote
\Delta
to be the diagonal of
X^{\sigma}\times X^{\sigma}
, and
\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)
to be the closure of
\operatorname{Attr}_{\sigma}(\Delta)
in
X\times X^{\sigma}
.

Consider the following condition on
(X,\mathsf{A},\sigma)
:

(
\star
)

for all
F^{\prime}\neq F\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\sigma}(X)
, we have

(4.7)

\displaystyle\dim\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)\cap(F^{\prime}\times F)<\dim F+\operatorname{rk}N^{+}_{F/X}-\operatorname{rk}N^{+}_{F^{\prime}/X}.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a chamber of
(X,\mathsf{A})
. We say that
(X,\mathsf{A},\mathfrak{C})
satisfies the condition (
\star
) if for a generic
\xi\in\mathfrak{C}
,
(X,\mathsf{A},\xi)
satisfies the condition (
\star
).

Remark 4.9
.

Assume moreover that
\dim N_{F/X}^{+}=\dim N_{F/X}^{-}
holds for all
F\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\mathsf{A}}(X)
, then (
4.7
) is equivalent to

(4.8)

\dim\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)\cap(F^{\prime}\times F)<\frac{1}{2}\dim(F^{\prime}\times F).

The condition
\dim N_{F/X}^{+}=\dim N_{F/X}^{-}
is satisfied when the tangent bundle has the following self-dual property:

\displaystyle T_{X}=T^{\vee}_{X}\in K^{\mathsf{A}}(X).

The
X
,
\widehat{X}
in Definition
4.1
satisfy this self-duality.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{T},\mathsf{A},\mathfrak{C})
be in the Setting
2.1
and assume that
(X,\mathsf{A},\mathfrak{C})
satisfies the condition (
\star
) in Definition
4.8
. Then

\sum_{F\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\mathsf{A}}(X)}[\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{F})]\in H^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\operatorname{Attr}^{f}_{\mathfrak{C}}\right)

is a stable envelope correspondence for
(X,0,\mathsf{T},\mathsf{A},\mathfrak{C})
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that for every
F\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\mathsf{A}}(X)
,
[\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{F})]
satisfies axioms (i) and (ii) in Definition
3.19
. The axiom (i) is obvious.
For axiom (ii), the class

(F^{\prime}\times F\hookrightarrow X\times F)^{*}[\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{F})]

is supported on a subvariety
\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{F})\cap(F^{\prime}\times F)
of dimension smaller than
\dim F+\operatorname{rk}N^{+}_{F/X}-\operatorname{rk}N^{+}_{F^{\prime}/X}
. Note that
\dim F+\operatorname{rk}N^{+}_{F/X}=\dim\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{F})
. Therefore

\displaystyle\deg_{\mathsf{A}}(F^{\prime}\times F\hookrightarrow X\times F)^{*}[\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{F})]

\displaystyle\leqslant\dim\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{F})\cap(F^{\prime}\times F)-\left(\dim\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{F})+\dim(F^{\prime}\times F)-\dim(X\times F)\right)

\displaystyle<\dim\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{F})-\operatorname{rk}N^{+}_{F^{\prime}/X}-\left(\dim\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{F})+\dim(F^{\prime}\times F)-\dim(X\times F)\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{rk}N^{-}_{F^{\prime}/X}.

This verifies the axiom (ii).
∎

4.4.
Generalities on family of smooth symplectic varieties

Let
(X,\mathsf{A})
be in the Setting
2.1
and let
\sigma
be a cocharacter of
\mathsf{A}
. Suppose that there exists an
\mathsf{A}
-invariant two-form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(X)
, and there exists a smooth connected variety
B
endowed with trivial
\mathsf{A}
-action and a smooth and
\mathsf{A}
-equivariant morphism
\phi\colon X\to B
, such that
\forall\,b\in B
, the restriction of
\omega
to
X_{b}:=\phi^{-1}(b)
is nondegenerate, i.e. a
symplectic structure
.

We note that
{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)\subseteq X\times_{B}X^{\sigma}
because
\Delta\subseteq X\times_{B}X^{\sigma}
and
\phi\colon X\to B
is
\mathsf{A}
-equivariant. Then it follows that
\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)\subseteq X\times_{B}X^{\sigma}
. We also have
\dim N_{F/X}^{+}=\dim N_{F/X}^{-}
holds for all
F\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\mathsf{A}}(X)
in this case.

Lemma 4.11
.

In the above situation, for all
F,F^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\sigma}(X)
and for all
b\in B
we have

\displaystyle\dim\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)\cap(F^{\prime}_{b}\times F_{b})\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\dim(F^{\prime}_{b}\times F_{b}),

where
F_{b}=F\cap\phi^{-1}(b)
and
F^{\prime}_{b}=F^{\prime}\cap\phi^{-1}(b)
.

Proof.

We consider the two-form
\omega^{\prime}:=\omega\boxminus\omega^{\sigma}
on
X\times X^{\sigma}
, then the restriction of
\omega^{\prime}
to every fiber
X_{b}\times X^{\sigma}_{b}
is a symplectic form. We claim that
\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)\cap(X_{b}\times X^{\sigma}_{b})
is an isotropic subvariety in
X_{b}\times X^{\sigma}_{b}
.
To prove this claim, first note that the restriction of
\omega^{\prime}
to
\Delta
is trivial by the construction of
\omega^{\prime}
. Since
\omega^{\prime}
is
\mathsf{A}
-invariant, its restriction to
{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)
must vanish as well. Thus the restriction of
\omega^{\prime}
to smooth locus of
\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)
vanishes by continuity. Let
W
be an irreducible component of
\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)\cap(X_{b}\times X^{\sigma}_{b})
. For a general point
w\in W
, there exists a sequence of points
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots
in the smooth locus of
\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)
approaching
w
such that limit of
T_{x_{i}}\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)
exists as
i\to\infty
and
contains the tangent space
T_{w}W

9
9

9

This can be seen by choosing a Whitney stratification of
\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)
for which
\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)\cap(X_{b}\times X^{\sigma}_{b})
is a union of strata.
. Since the restriction of
\omega^{\prime}
to
T_{x_{i}}\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)
vanishes, our claim follows.

Then it follows that
\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)\cap(F^{\prime}_{b}\times F_{b})
is an isotropic subvariety in
F^{\prime}_{b}\times F_{b}
by
[
MO
, Lem. 3.4.1]
. Thus

\dim\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)\cap(F^{\prime}_{b}\times F_{b})\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\dim(F^{\prime}_{b}\times F_{b}).\qed

Lemma 4.12
.

In the same situation as Lemma
4.11
, assume moreover that there exists an open dense subset
B^{\circ}\subseteq B
such that
\phi|_{X^{\circ}}\colon X^{\circ}\to B^{\circ}
is a quasi-affine morphism where
X^{\circ}:=\phi^{-1}(B^{\circ})
. Then
(X,\mathsf{A},\sigma)
satisfies the condition (
\star
) (
4.7
).

Proof.

For every
x\in X^{\sigma}
, the tangent map
d\phi_{x}\colon T_{x}X\to T_{\phi(x)}B
is surjective by the assumption that
\phi\colon X\to B
is smooth.
d\phi_{x}
is also
\sigma
-equivariant since
\phi
is
\sigma
-equivariant. Then it follows that
d\phi_{x}
maps
T_{x}X^{\sigma}=(T_{x}X)^{\sigma}
surjectively onto
T_{\phi(x)}B
, so the restriction of
\phi
to
\sigma
-fixed loci
\phi^{\sigma}\colon X^{\sigma}\to B
is also smooth. In particular,
\phi^{\sigma}
is flat. The flatness of
\phi^{\sigma}
implies that
\forall\,F\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\sigma}(X)
and any
b\in\phi(F)
,
we have

\dim F=\dim F_{b}+\dim B.

For
F^{\prime}\neq F\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\sigma}(X)
, let
F^{\prime\circ}:=F^{\prime}\cap X^{\circ}
and
F^{\circ}:=F\cap X^{\circ}
. We claim that

\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)\cap(F^{\prime\circ}\times F^{\circ})=\emptyset.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
B^{\circ}
is affine, then
X^{\circ}
and
(X^{\circ})^{\sigma}
are quasi-affine. Let
\Delta^{\circ}
be the diagonal of
(X^{\circ})^{\sigma}\times(X^{\circ})^{\sigma}
, then
{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta^{\circ})
is closed in
X^{\circ}\times(X^{\circ})^{\sigma}

10
10

10

Taking a finite set of
\mathsf{A}
-homogeneous generators of
\mathbb{C}[X^{\circ}\times(X^{\circ})^{\sigma}]
, we get a surjective
\mathsf{A}
-equivariant algebra map
\mathbb{C}[V]\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{C}[X^{\circ}\times(X^{\circ})^{\sigma}]
where
V
is a vector space with a linear
\mathsf{A}
-action. Equivalently we get an
\mathsf{A}
-equivariant closed embedding
X^{\circ}\times(X^{\circ})^{\sigma}\hookrightarrow V
. We note that
\operatorname{Attr}_{\sigma}(V^{\sigma})=V^{\geqslant 0}
where
V^{\geqslant 0}
is the linear subspace of
V
spanned by nonnegative
\sigma
-eigenvectors, in particular
\operatorname{Attr}_{\sigma}(V^{\sigma})
is closed in
V
. We also note that
{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta^{\circ})=p^{-1}(\Delta^{\circ})\cap(X^{\circ}\times(X^{\circ})^{\sigma})
, where
p\colon V^{\geqslant 0}\to V^{\sigma}
is the attraction map and
\Delta^{\circ}
is regarded as a closed subset of
V^{\sigma}
. Thus
{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta^{\circ})
is closed in
X^{\circ}\times(X^{\circ})^{\sigma}
.
. Therefore

\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)\cap(X^{\circ}\times(X^{\circ})^{\sigma})={\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta^{\circ}),

hence
\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)\cap(F^{\prime\circ}\times F^{\circ})=\emptyset
. In the general case we can cover
B^{\circ}
with affine open subsets and apply the above argument to each of the affine open subset. This proves our claim.

Finally, we have the following dimension bound:

\displaystyle\dim\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)\cap(F^{\prime}\times F)

\displaystyle\leqslant\dim(B\setminus B^{\circ})+\sup_{b\in B}\dim\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\sigma}(\Delta)\cap(F^{\prime}_{b}\times F_{b})

By Lem.
4.11

\displaystyle\leqslant\dim(B\setminus B^{\circ})+\sup_{b\in B}\frac{1}{2}\dim(F^{\prime}_{b}\times F_{b})

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\dim(F^{\prime}\times F)-1.

This shows that
(X,\mathsf{A},\sigma)
satisfies the condition (
\star
) (
4.7
).
∎

4.5.
Proof of Theorem
4.3

We first show that the attracting closure for the diagonal of
\widehat{X}
in Definition
4.1

is a stable envelope correspondence.

Lemma 4.13
.

\sum_{F\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\mathsf{A}}(\widehat{X})}[\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{F})]\in H^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\operatorname{Attr}^{f}_{\mathfrak{C}}\right)

is a stable envelope correspondence for
(\widehat{X},0,\mathsf{T},\mathsf{A},\mathfrak{C})
.

Proof.

Since
R\cong R^{\vee}
as
H
-representations, we have an isomorphism of
H
-representations:

R\cong T^{*}N\oplus R_{0},

where
N=\oplus_{\alpha\neq 0}\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}
and
R_{0}
is a trivial
H
-representation. In particular, there is a moment map

\mu\colon T^{*}N\to\mathfrak{h}^{*}.

Define
\nu\colon R=T^{*}N\times R_{0}\xrightarrow{\mu\times\operatorname{id}}\mathfrak{h}^{*}\times R_{0}
, then the restriction of
\nu
to
R^{{\theta}\emph{-}s}
is smooth. By passing to the quotient, we obtain a smooth map

\bar{\nu}\colon\widehat{X}\to\mathfrak{h}^{*}\times R_{0},

and for any
x\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}\times R_{0}
,
\bar{\nu}^{-1}(x)
is a smooth symplectic variety.

For a generic
x\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}\times R_{0}
, we have
{\nu}^{-1}(x)\subseteq R^{\bar{\theta}\emph{-}s}
; therefore
\bar{\nu}^{-1}(x)
is an open subset of
{\nu}^{-1}(x)/\!\!/H
, in particular
\bar{\nu}^{-1}(x)
is quasi-affine.
By Lemma
4.12
, we know
(
4.8
) holds for
\widehat{X}=R^{{\theta}\emph{-}s}\times_{G}(G/H)
in Definition
4.1
.
By applying Lemma
4.10
, we are done.
∎

Next we want to relate the geometry of
\widehat{X}
and
X
. We define
\overline{X}:=R^{s}\times_{G}(G/B)
and have fibrations:

p\colon\widehat{X}\xrightarrow{p_{1}}\overline{X}\xrightarrow{p_{2}}X.

For any
S\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\mathsf{A}}(X)
, which is given by
S=\left(R^{\mathsf{A},\phi}\cap R^{{\theta}\emph{-}s}\right)/G^{\phi(\mathsf{A})}
for a group homomorphism
\phi\colon\mathsf{A}\to G
, we define

\widehat{S}:=\left(R^{\mathsf{A},\phi}\cap R^{{\theta}\emph{-}s}\right)\times_{G^{\phi(\mathsf{A})}}\left(G^{\phi(\mathsf{A})}/H\right)\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\mathsf{A}}(\widehat{X}),\quad\overline{S}:=\left(R^{\mathsf{A},\phi}\cap R^{{\theta}\emph{-}s}\right)\times_{G^{\phi(\mathsf{A})}}\left(G^{\phi(\mathsf{A})}/B^{\phi(\mathsf{A})}\right)\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\mathsf{A}}(\overline{X}),

with natural morphisms

q\colon\widehat{S}\xrightarrow{q_{1}}\overline{S}\xrightarrow{q_{2}}S.

Proposition 4.14
.

For any
S,S^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Fix}_{\mathsf{A}}(X)
with
S\neq S^{\prime}
, we have

\deg_{\mathsf{A}}\left(\left(S^{\prime}\times S\hookrightarrow X\times S\right)^{!}[\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{S})]\right)<\operatorname{rk}N_{S^{\prime}/X}^{-}.

In particular, this finishes the proof of Theorem
4.3
.

Proof.

Let
Z:=\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{S})
,
\overline{Z}:=(p_{2}\times q_{2})^{-1}(Z)
,
\widehat{Z}:=(p\times q)^{-1}(Z)
. Then

\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{\widehat{S}})\subseteq\widehat{Z}.

By Weyl conjugation, we choose
\phi:\mathsf{A}\to G
such that
N_{\overline{S}/p_{2}^{-1}(S)}
are repelling in chamber
\mathfrak{C}
.
We have

(p_{1}\times q_{1})^{!}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}}(\overline{Z})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{\mathsf{T}}(\widehat{Z}),\quad(p_{2}\times q_{2})_{*}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}}(\overline{Z})\to H^{\mathsf{T}}(Z).

Here
p_{1},q_{1}
are
U
-fibrations where
B=H\cdot U
and
U
is a unipotent group,
p_{2},q_{2}
are proper maps as
G/B
is proper.

Consider the composition:

(4.9)

\eta:=\eta_{\widehat{X}\times\widehat{S}\to X\times S}:=(p_{2}\times q_{2})_{*}\circ\left((p_{1}\times q_{1})^{!}\right)^{-1}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}}(\widehat{Z})\to H^{\mathsf{T}}({Z}).

By
\mathsf{A}
-localization, we have

\displaystyle\quad\,\,\left(S^{\prime}\times S\hookrightarrow X\times S\right)^{!}\eta_{\widehat{X}\times\widehat{S}\to X\times S}\left([\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{\widehat{S}})]\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\widehat{S^{\prime}_{i}}\to S^{\prime}}\eta_{\widehat{S^{\prime}_{i}}\times\widehat{S}\to S^{\prime}\times S}\left(\frac{1}{e^{\mathsf{T}}\left(N_{\overline{S^{\prime}_{i}}/p_{2}^{-1}(S^{\prime})}\right)}\left(\widehat{S^{\prime}_{i}}\times\widehat{S}\hookrightarrow\widehat{X}\times\widehat{S}\right)^{!}[\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{\widehat{S}})]\right),

where the sum is taken over all
\mathsf{A}
-fixed components
\{\widehat{S^{\prime}_{i}}\}_{i}
of
p^{-1}(S^{\prime})
. Therefore by Lemma
4.13
, we have

\displaystyle\deg_{\mathsf{A}}\left(S^{\prime}\times S\hookrightarrow X\times S\right)^{!}\eta_{\widehat{X}\times\widehat{S}\to X\times S}\left([\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{\widehat{S}})]\right)

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{rk}N_{\widehat{S^{\prime}_{i}}/\widehat{X}}-\operatorname{rk}N_{\overline{S^{\prime}_{i}}/p_{2}^{-1}(S^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{rk}N_{\widehat{S^{\prime}_{i}}/\widehat{X}}-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{rk}N_{\widehat{S^{\prime}_{i}}/p^{-1}(S^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{rk}N_{S^{\prime}/X}.

Finally, Lemma
4.15
below finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.15
.

In (
4.9
), we have

\eta\left([\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{\widehat{S}})]\right)=|W^{\phi}|\cdot(-1)^{\sharp}\cdot[\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{{S}})].

Proof.

By dimension counting, we have
\eta\left([\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{\widehat{S}})]\right)\in H^{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathrm{top}}(Z)
; therefore

\eta\left([\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{\widehat{S}})]\right)=\lambda\cdot[\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{S})],

for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}
, which we compute by restricting above to
S\times S
. By
\mathsf{A}
-localization, we have

\displaystyle\left(S\times S\hookrightarrow X\times S\right)^{!}\eta_{\widehat{X}\times\widehat{S}\to X\times S}\left([\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\Delta_{\widehat{S}})]\right)

\displaystyle=\eta_{\widehat{S}\times\widehat{S}\to S\times S}\left(\frac{[\Delta(\widehat{S})]}{e^{\mathsf{T}}\left(N_{\overline{S}/p_{2}^{-1}(S)}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=(p_{2}\times p_{2})_{*}\left(\frac{e^{\mathsf{T}}\left(N^{\vee}_{\overline{S}/p_{2}^{-1}(S)}\right)}{e^{\mathsf{T}}\left(N_{\overline{S}/p_{2}^{-1}(S)}\right)}\cdot[\Delta(\widehat{S})]\right)

\displaystyle=|W^{\phi}|\cdot(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}N_{\overline{S}/p_{2}^{-1}(S)}}\cdot[\Delta(S)].

This implies that
\lambda=|W^{\phi}|\cdot(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}N_{\overline{S}/p_{2}^{-1}(S)}}
and we are done.
∎

4.6.
Triangle lemma for cohomological stable envelopes

Let
X=R/\!\!/_{\theta}G
be a symmetric GIT quotient and
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\mathsf{T}
be the tori as in Definition
4.1
. We fix a chamber
\mathfrak{C}
and a face
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
, and let
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{A}
be the subtorus associated with
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
. Then
X^{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}
is disjoint union of symmetric GIT quotients, so there exists cohomological stable envelope correspondence for
(X^{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}},0,\mathsf{T},\mathsf{A},\mathfrak{C}/\mathfrak{C}^{\prime})
by Theorem
4.3
. Consider the induced stable envelopes for a
\mathsf{T}
-invariant function
\mathsf{w}
on
X
, and we have the following triangle lemma.

Theorem 4.16
.

Using the above notation, the following diagram is commutative

\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(X^{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{w}^{\mathsf{A}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}/\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})}
\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(X^{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}},\mathsf{w}^{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}}

Theorem
4.16
will be proven in steps.

Step 1.
We formulate a nonabelian stable envelope for symmetric quotient stack
\mathfrak{X}=[R/G]
, to this end, we will need the following lemma.

Lemma 4.17
.

Consider the closure of the diagonal
\Delta_{X}
in
X\times\mathfrak{X}
, denoted
\overline{\Delta_{X}}
. Then the projection from
\overline{\Delta_{X}}
to
\mathfrak{X}
is representable and proper.

Proof.

We have the following explicit construction of
\overline{\Delta_{X}}
as a quotient stack. Consider the graph

\mathrm{Graph}(q)\subset X\times R^{\theta\emph{-}s}

of quotient map
q\colon R^{\theta\emph{-}s}\to X
, and take the closure of
\mathrm{Graph}(q)
in
X\times R
,
denoted by
\overline{\mathrm{Graph}(q)}
. Then

\overline{\Delta_{X}}=[\overline{\mathrm{Graph}(q)}/G].

This implies that
\overline{\Delta_{X}}\to\mathfrak{X}
is representable. Take
X_{0}=R/\!\!/G=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[R]^{G}
to be the affine quotient, then the natural projection
\pi\colon X\to X_{0}
is projective. Note that the composition
\pi\circ q\colon R^{\theta\emph{-}s}\to X_{0}
extends to the quotient map
Q\colon R\to X_{0}
, so
\pi\times\operatorname{id}\colon X\times R\to X_{0}\times R
maps
\overline{\mathrm{Graph}(q)}
to
\mathrm{Graph}(Q)
. In particular, the projection
\overline{\mathrm{Graph}(q)}\to R
factors as a proper morphism
\overline{\mathrm{Graph}(q)}\to\mathrm{Graph}(Q)
followed by an isomorphism
\mathrm{Graph}(Q)\cong R
; thus
\overline{\mathrm{Graph}(q)}\to R
is proper. This implies that
\overline{\Delta_{X}}\to\mathfrak{X}
is representable and proper.
∎

Since the function
\mathsf{w}\boxminus\mathsf{w}
vanishes on
\overline{\Delta_{X}}
, the fundamental class
[\overline{\Delta_{X}}]
induces a correspondence map
between critical cohomologies.

Definition 4.18
.

We call the induced map

(4.10)

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Psi}_{H}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})\to H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathsf{w})

by
[\overline{\Delta_{X}}]
, a (cohomological)
nonabelian stable envelope
.

It turns out that
\mathbf{\Psi}_{H}
admits a
characterization
in terms of a degree bound condition similar to that of stable envelopes. For any nonzero cocharacter
\sigma:\mathbb{C}^{*}\to G
, let
R^{\sigma}\subset R
be the fixed subspace with respect to the induced
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action and
G^{\sigma}\subset G
be the
\sigma
-fixed subgroup of
G
. Define
\mathfrak{X}^{\sigma}:=[R^{\sigma}/G^{\sigma}]
, and denote

\mathbf{j}_{\sigma}:\mathfrak{X}^{\sigma}\to\mathfrak{X}

the natural map between quotient stacks.

Theorem 4.19
.

\mathbf{\Psi}_{H}
is the unique
H_{\mathsf{T}}(\operatorname{pt})
-linear map from
H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})
to
H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathsf{w})
that satisfies the following:

•

\mathrm{res}\circ\mathbf{\Psi}_{H}=\operatorname{id}
, where
\mathrm{res}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathsf{w})\to H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})
is the map induced by restriction from
\mathfrak{X}
to the open substack
X
,

•

for all nonzero cocharacter
\sigma:\mathbb{C}^{*}\to G
, the inequality

(4.11)

\displaystyle\deg_{\sigma}\mathbf{j}_{\sigma}^{*}\mathbf{\Psi}_{H}(\gamma)<\frac{1}{2}(\dim\mathfrak{X}-\dim\mathfrak{X}^{\sigma})

holds for all
\gamma\in H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})
. Here
\deg_{\sigma}
is the polynomial degree in
H_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\operatorname{pt})
in the decomposition
H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathfrak{X}^{\sigma},\mathsf{w})\cong H^{\mathsf{T}\times G^{\sigma}/\sigma(\mathbb{C}^{*})}(R^{\sigma},\mathsf{w})\otimes H_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\operatorname{pt})
.

Theorem
4.19

will be used in the proof of Theorem
4.22
below, and will be proven in Section
4.7
.

Step 2.
We introduce a Hall operation relating critical cohomology of “
\mathsf{A}
-fixed” stack of
\mathfrak{X}
to that of
\mathfrak{X}
, and compare it with the stable envelope. Take a group homomorphism

\phi:\mathsf{A}\to G,

define
R^{\mathsf{A},\phi}
to be the
\mathsf{A}
-fixed subspace of
R
where
\mathsf{A}
acts via
\mathsf{A}\xrightarrow{(\operatorname{id},\phi)}\mathsf{A}\times G\curvearrowright R
, and define

\mathfrak{X}^{\mathsf{A},\phi}:=[R^{\mathsf{A},\phi}/G^{\phi(\mathsf{A})}],

where
G^{\phi(\mathsf{A})}
is the subgroup of
G
fixed by adjoint action of
\phi(\mathsf{A})
. We assume that the stable locus
R^{\mathsf{A},\phi}\cap R^{\theta\emph{-}s}
is nonempty, so the GIT quotient
X^{\mathsf{A},\phi}:=R^{\mathsf{A},\phi}/\!\!/_{\theta}G^{\phi(\mathsf{A})}
is a connected component of
\mathsf{A}
-fixed loci
X^{\mathsf{A}}
. Note that all connected components of
X^{\mathsf{A}}
arises in this way, and we have

(4.12)

\mathfrak{X}^{\mathsf{A}}=\bigsqcup_{\phi/\sim}\mathfrak{X}^{\mathsf{A},\phi},

where the sum is taken for all homomorphisms
\phi:\mathsf{A}\to G
modulo the equivalence relation:
\phi_{1}\sim\phi_{2}
if and only if they give the isomorphic
(G\times\mathsf{A})
-module structures on
R
.

We define a map
\mathfrak{m}^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathfrak{X}^{\mathsf{A},\phi},\mathsf{w})\to H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathsf{w})
, according to a choice of chamber
\mathfrak{C}
, as follows. Let

L^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}:=\operatorname{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(R^{\mathsf{A},\phi})\subset R,\quad P^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}:=\operatorname{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(G^{\phi(\mathsf{A})})\subset G,

where
L^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is a linear subspace of
R
, and

is a parabolic subgroup of
G
.
There are maps

\textstyle{R^{\mathsf{A},\phi}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces L^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{R,}

and maps

Therefore we get a diagram

\textstyle{\mathfrak{X}^{\mathsf{A},\phi}}

\textstyle{\mathfrak{X},}

where

\mathfrak{p}

Definition 4.20
.

We define the
Hall operation

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}:=\mathfrak{p}_{*}\circ\mathfrak{q}^{*}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathfrak{X}^{\mathsf{A},\phi},\mathsf{w})\longrightarrow H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathsf{w}).

Remark 4.21
.

Let
\widetilde{L}^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}:=G\times^{P^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}}{L}^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}
and
\widetilde{p}:\widetilde{L}^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}\to R
be the natural morphism induced by the

\displaystyle\mathrm{ind}^{G}_{P^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}\times P^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}}({L}^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}},\mathsf{w})\cong H^{\mathsf{T}\times G}(\widetilde{L}^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}},\mathsf{w}).

Note that the inverse of

is given by

, where

\mathrm{res}^{G}_{P^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}}
is the base change functor that takes

-equivariant counterpart. Using the Cartesian diagram

\textstyle{\widetilde{L}^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{p}}
\scriptstyle{\cdot/G}
\scriptstyle{\Box}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot/G}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{L}^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{p}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{X},}

the Hall operation can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}=\widetilde{p}_{*}\circ\mathrm{ind}^{G}_{P^{\phi}_{\mathfrak{C}}}\circ\mathfrak{q}^{*}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}\times G^{\phi(\mathsf{A})}}(R^{\mathsf{A},\phi},\mathsf{w})\longrightarrow H^{\mathsf{T}\times G}(R,\mathsf{w}).

Theorem 4.22
.

The following diagram is commutative

(4.14)

\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathfrak{X}^{\mathsf{A},\phi},\mathsf{w})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathsf{w})}
\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(X^{\mathsf{A},\phi},\mathsf{w})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w}),\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

.

We will prove Theorem

in Section

.

Step 3.
To finish the proof of Theorem
4.16
, consider the following diagram on a triangular prism:

\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathfrak{X}^{\mathsf{A},\phi},\mathsf{w})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathsf{w})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
